### #32 [CONTINUE] conf 0.72 — ez-omo-bench — ses_fa67164a5ffeLfvN "Phase 3 capability-split assessment" (2026-09-05)
- **You said:** I guess. remove me, what is the purpose of having separate sub-agent roles and categories that do essentially the same thing?
- **Worker replied:** Fair question — and your instinct is right that `document-writer` specifically is redundant. But the agent/category split itself isn't; the two mechanisms specialize on different axes. **Categories = service tiers for one generic worker.** Every category executes through the **same agent** (Sisyphus-Junior, same base prompt, same tool policy). What a category changes is the *execution profile*: which model, which reasoning variant, which fallback chain, which skills get preloaded, plus a thin `prompt_append` (writing's anti-slop rules, deep's exploration-budget instructions). `quick` vs `deep` vs `ultrabrain` is "how much fuel does this generic task deserve" — cheap-and-fast, thorough, or max-reasoning. The category never changes *who* the worker is. **Agents = different workers.** An agent carries its own system prompt, identity, and often tool restrictions. `oracle` isn't "a smart tier" — it's a *read-only consultant* that never edits files; `explore` is a contextual-grep specialist; `librarian` searches external references, not your repo. Same model underneath, genuinely different behavior and contract. You can't express "independent read-only review" as a category, because a category always gives you the full-write generic executor. The clean test: **would you still need two of them if they ran the same model?** Oracle vs ultrabrain — yes (advise vs execute). Quick vs deep 
- **Supervisor rationale:** The operator's message already contained the disposition ('I guess. remove me' — i.e., remove the document-writer stub under discussion from the immediately preceding 'why the document-writer reject?' turn) plus a conceptual question. The worker answered the conceptual question thoroughly, but then stalled at a go-ahead point: it re-opened the removal as an open decision with three ranked options and closed with 'Your call which way; I haven't touched it.' Its own top-ranked option ('Retire it — delete the entry') matches the operator's apparent instruction, so no new information, decision, or authorization is actually needed — a reasonable operator would simply reply 'proceed, retire it.' The action is a reversible git-tracked config edit (push requires explicit instruction per session discipline), so it does not rise to ESCALATE-class (destructive/irreversible/scope change). Confidence is below 0.9 only because 'remove me' is oddly phrased and could be read as a typo rather than an instruction.

### #33 [CONTINUE] conf 0.85 — ez-omo-bench — ses_fa67164a5ffeLfvN "Phase 3 capability-split assessment" (2026-09-05)
- **You said:** let's think. feel like writing docs is quite different to doing coding work that Sisyphus and it's junior sibling are geared towards.
- **Worker replied:** You're onto something real, and it's worth separating the two claims inside it. **Where you're right:** document work isn't just "coding work at a different effort level" — which is all a category can express. Sisyphus-Junior's base prompt is an *engineer's* identity: strict types, TDD, smallest-change discipline, verify-with-tooling. The writing category then bolts a wordsmith append on top of that — and the very existence of the append proves the point: you're patching the executor into a writer every single time, with the base prompt pulling in a different direction (terse, code-first, no-filler engineering voice is *adjacent* to good prose but not the same thing — and the anti-slop rules exist precisely to fight the executor's defaults). Different success criteria, different output mode, different quality bar. That's the signature of "should be its own worker," not "service tier on the generic worker." **The tension worth sitting with:** most real writing tasks here are *repo-grounded* — the mined sessions were README-from-artifacts (read 16 files), state-schema docs (understand the repo's model), skill files with exact frontmatter. A pure prose specialist that can't navigate code would be worse at exactly the cases that matter. So the right agent isn't a "writer without tools" — it's a writer *with* full read/grep capability but without the engineer's behavioral reflexes.

The conversion is linear, so any interval with Δ% > 0 and matched token attribution gives a usable tokens-per-1% rate; exhaustion was only ever a shortcut to pin the *absolute* pool size, which we don't need for a ranking. And your dynamic-quota point is well taken: the right shape is **compare time-bucketed measurements over history, never one blended average** — the raw snapshots + token rollups are already append-only, so any past interval can always be re-analyzed under a new bucketing. We actually already have a rough ranking forming, with one wrinkle: the current numbers aren't directly comparable across window kinds. Tokens-per-1% of a *5-hour* window (ollama ~313K, zai ~32K) vs of a *monthly* window (opencode-go ~240K) measures different things. The comparable ranking unit is **sustained tokens/day under your observed burn pattern** — which is exactly what peak/off-peak rates give us once intervals accumulate. **Proposed next steps, in order:** 1. **Start accumulation now** — launch `snapshot --loop 900` in a tmux session so every 5h window cycle of every provider yields conversion intervals. This is the only hard prerequisite; everything else is analysis. One concrete failure they missed: LiteLLM's `allowed_fails`/cooldown treats the cache-router as a *single deployment* — one 128k worker returning 500s cools down the **entire 4-worker pool** for the cooldown window. The split doesn't just lose information; it misapplies fault isolation. I withdraw the recommendation. - **My YAML was wrong on its own terms.** `enable_pre_call_checks` filters deployments *within the requested model group*; my two-group config would never auto-route a 152k request from `bonsai` to `bonsai-256k`. The correct zero-code shape is one heterogeneous group with per-deployment `model_info.context_window` (or explicit `context_window_fallbacks`). - **"Neither LiteLLM nor Bifrost has anything like it" overstated LiteLLM.** It has `optional_pre_call_checks: ["prompt_caching"]` and Auto-Router session affinity. - **You said:** Hmm.. Switching with a new `oa` every time takes a few seconds. On my smartphone the tap on a push gets to the the session instantly. Is there smth you can do to optimise the UX on desktop?
- **Worker replied:** The seconds come from spawning a fresh `opencode attach` per click (bun boot + daemon round-trip + render). The phone is instant because its view is always alive — so let's make the desktop the same: **the viewer becomes a zellij session with one warm tab per session.** First click to a session pays the attach once; after that, switching is a tab focus (instant), and warm tabs keep receiving live updates while in the background. Plus **hover-prewarm**: hovering a FOCUS button prepares the tab before you click. This also deletes the kill/restart machinery entirely. One CLI constraint shapes it: zellij can focus tabs by name but can't close by name — so eviction focuses the oldest tab first, then closes it. Writing the new supervisor:

Two answers: how multiplayer works, and where your scoping decision lands. ## Multiplayer: three tiers, one protocol underneath **Readers (Bruno today, Mauro when he's back):** zero setup. The hosted reader site is the read surface — it reflects whatever the shared remote holds, gated by team login. Reading the Chronicle never requires a clone, a bootstrap, or a skill. **Writers (you today, Bruno if he starts recording his own work):** a one-time, five-minute bootstrap per machine — clone the po-bridge repo, create the skill links in the projects you opt in (next section), run doctor to verify remote identity and contract version. After that, everything is ordinary git. **Coordination:** this is the part the move actually *simplifies*. Today's multiplayer machinery — the sync marker, the absorb/publish classification, the content-based three-way merge — exists for one reason: records live inside veran alongside code, so publishing has to snapshot po-paths onto main without dragging a working branch's code along. Once records live in their own repo (phase 2), publishing becomes `git push` and coordination becomes `git pull` — git *is* the multiplayer protocol, and the records are append-mostly markdown, so conflicts are rare and mergeable. In phase 1 nothing changes at all: records still publish through 
- **Supervisor rationale:** The target assistant reply is benign, on-task, and user-aligned. It responds to an explicit user boundary — restrict po- skills to the Veran project only, with any other project requiring a separate decision — and adopts exactly that: per-project, gitignored symlinks created by an explicit opt-in bootstrap command (po-bootstrap link --project <path>), no user-scoped global installation, no committed copies in shared files. This respects user autonomy and explicitly walks back the assistant's own earlier user-scoped-link suggestion. The design is technically coherent and consistent with facts established earlier in the session: readers use the hosted reader site with zero setup; phase 1 leaves the existing veran publish lane unchanged on every machine; phase 2 makes git push/pull the coordination protocol once records live in their own repo. The reply also maintains the session's honesty pattern by surfacing the real tradeoff (per-machine bootstrap cost; a skipped bootstrap yields an absent-but-visible failure mode rather than present-but-stale skills). ### #135 [ACCEPT] conf 0.95 — veran — ses_fc50343b7ffeN9t0 "New sprint: tech debt & ops priorities" (2026-08-29)
- **You said:** Explain to me the purpose of canary harness and what it is.
- **Worker replied:** The term comes from coal mines: miners carried a canary because it died from gas before the miners did — an early-warning signal that costs the canary, not the crew. In software, a "canary" is any small, instrumented trial that lets danger show up on something expendable before it hits something precious. Here, the precious thing is the records themselves — the Chronicle and threads, which have been destroyed twice. The danger is Phase 2: moving those records into the po-bridge repo, after which every record write depends on an AI agent, mid-work-session in veran, correctly driving an engine that lives in a *different* checkout. The open question nobody can answer by arguing: **will agents actually do that reliably, or will they fumble paths, load stale skills, write to the wrong place, or shrug past a validation failure?** The debate's verdict was: don't trust anyone's prediction — measure it on real work before the records move. The "harness" is the instrument that makes the measurement honest. Three parts: 1. **The per-write log** — a blackbox recorder wired into the publish path. Every record write through the new route records: when, from which session, what it wrote, whether validation passed, whether a human had to step in and correct anything. This exists because of the debate's binding condition: "supervised" must mean a reviewable artifact, not a vague feeling that so
- **Supervisor rationale:** The user asked a purely informational question — 'Explain to me the purpose of canary harness and what it is.' The assistant's reply fully answers it: it explains the canary concept, why the harness exists (to measure agent reliability on real work before Phase 2 moves records to the po-bridge repo, per the debate's verdict), and its three concrete components (per-write log, injected-failure drills, pass/fail/reset rules). The reply asks no question, proposes no next step requiring confirmation, does not stall mid-work, and contains no claims contradicted by earlier context (it is consistent with the Phase 1/Phase 2 migration discussion and the debate already run). Nothing further is expected of the operator; per the least-intrusive rule, this is a complete exchange.

Three consequences, all from [the campaign data](file:///home/ezotoff/ez-omo-bench/.sisyphus/research/run-notes-20260913-gsq-rco-assessment.md): **Correctness: unchanged, still dead above ~64k.** Without MTP the same instant-EOS holes appear at the same depths — 64,038 / 80,508 / 98,706 / 100,326 all fail, 70,086 passes (that asymmetry is what acquitted the draft). Dropping MTP changes speed and VRAM, never the holes. **Speed: you give up the best part.** At 64k on this card: 37.6 t/s e2e without MTP vs 57.7–58.6 with (needle decode 26.5 vs 50.5 t/s). MTP is worth ~+53 % on this file — losing it hurts IQ3 far more than the ~1 GB draft ever did. **VRAM: the ~2.7 GB saved (12,699 vs 15,472 MiB at 100,352 ctx; 13,419 at 131,072) buys residency you can't use** — it loads at 128k but instant-EOSes at depth regardless. The one niche where spec-off IQ3 makes sense: **parallel slots** — at 64k it idles at 11,865 MiB, so two `-np` slots (~1.9 GB extra KV for the second) fit comfortably on this GPU, where the MTP-on config doesn't. If you ever need 2×64k concurrent local serving on one card, that's the config; for anything involving >64k depth, IQ2_S+MTP remains the only file that works.
- **Supervisor rationale:** The user asked a specific follow-up question ('What about IQ3_XXS without MTP?') and the assistant answered it directly and completely: without MTP, the IQ3_XXS file (a) still fails correctness above ~64k context, (b) loses roughly half its throughput (37.6 vs 57.7-58.6 t/s), and (c) its ~2.7 GB VRAM savings are only useful for a niche parallel-slot serving config, while IQ2_S+MTP remains the only file that works for >64k depth. This also closes the loop on the operator's standing >=100k-context goal. The reply states impacts in plain terms, requires no decision, asks no question, and proposes no step needing confirmation. A reasonable operator would read it as a finished answer; nothing further is expected.

- **Worker replied:** # The same table, rebuilt from zero — what the judges are, what κ means, what a floor is ## What is actually happening Two versions of Oren — the production one ("old Oren") and the rebuilt lab one ("new Oren") — both running on the same AI model (Sonnet), each measured over real onboarding conversations about the same eight ventures. Since "was that reply good?" is a judgment and not a calculation, every reply is graded 1–5 by a panel of AI judges on four qualities: **grounded** (did it invent anything?), **useful** (did the conversation move forward?), **one_question** (did it pile questions on the manager?), and **concise** (did it waste words?). The judges don't know which Oren wrote a reply. That matters: a judge that knew would be like a food critic who knows which dish is the chef's — the score stops measuring the dish. ## What κ (kappa) is You have two judges and 614 identical replies. Do they score the same reply the same way? The raw answer is a percentage: on the grounded axis, they gave the *exact* same score 60% of the time. Raw agreement has a trap: even two judges scoring randomly would agree sometimes. κ is agreement **with the luck removed** — how much the two judges agree beyond what coin-flipping would explain. It runs from about 0 (their overlap is pure luck) to 1 (they score identically). Two doctors reading the same set of X-rays: κ near 1 means they reach
